Typhon's nature

Urth list · 167 messages · 2011-10-09 to 2011-10-20

2011urthThe Book of the New SunThe Book of the Long SunLane HaygoodMark MillmanJerry FriedmanLee BermanTim O'DonnellAntónio Pedro MarquesGerry QuinnDavid StockhoffJames WynnMarc AraminiLarry MillerJeff WilsonDan'l Danehy-OakesAndrew Mason
The thread: Participants debated Typhon's nature in Wolfe's Long Sun and Short Sun books: whether he is an alien, a cloned or engineered human, a Neighbour such as Windcloud, or a sea monster's kin, and how his telepathy works. Larry Miller and Jeff Wilson argued for alien and technological readings, while David Stockhoff and Gerry Quinn pressed for genetic or human-built explanations; Andrew Mason disputed the Neighbour link and the Nessus chronology. Positions shifted repeatedly without settling. The thread later degenerated into a dispute over personal attacks, with Mark Millman and David Stockhoff trading accusations and little textual analysis.
  • Larry Miller: Typhon is an alien, possibly an original megatherian with a bean-grown body
  • David Stockhoff: Typhon is a human-built figure, with genetic telepathy and a cloned or surgically assembled nature
  • Jeff Wilson: Typhon uses orbital sensors, automated listening posts or impressed brain patterns rather than innate telepathy
  • Gerry Quinn: Typhon looked human and needed only a body transplant; telepathy blends with charisma
  • Andrew Mason: Typhon is not a Neighbour, since Wolfe said he did not know about the inhumi; the Nessus passage conflicts with dating
  • Jerry Friedman: Typhon's psionics are plausible for humans in Briah, possibly engineered

Themes: Beasts and the animal in us, Robots, androids and artificial life, Genre: science fiction, fantasy, both

2011-10-09 Larry Miller replying to Andrew Mason #

Proposes that Typhon in The Sword of the Lictor is an alien, citing his claim that he was not born in the usual way and his sending of thoughts to distant places, as similar to the Neighbors' astral abilities. Suggests Cillinia may have a saint's name because Piaton fathered her and an alien Monarch later adopted her, responding to Larry Miller's account of saints' names.
proposes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon is an alien rather than human; Cillinia was fathered by Piaton and adopted by the alien Monarch

In the Sword of the Lictor Typhon says a few things that seems to indicate an alien origin. He tells Severian that "I was not born, at least not in the way you mean it". Also Typhon says that though he has lived a day he has sent his thoughts to far places, seemingly making contact with the megatherians. This seems very similiar to the astral abilities possessed by the Nieghbors. Was Cillinia born on the Whorl or Urth? Perhaps she has a saints name because she was fathered by Piaton and then later adopted by the alien Monarch?

Whole message, with quotes · original
In the Sword of the Lictor Typhon says a few things that seems to
indicate an alien origin. He tells  Severian that "I was not born, at
least not in the way you mean it".   Also Typhon says that though he
has lived a day he has sent his thoughts to far places, seemingly
making contact with the megatherians.  This seems very similiar to the
astral abilities possessed by the Nieghbors.  Was Cillinia born on the
Whorl or Urth?  Perhaps she has a saints name because she was fathered
by Piaton and then later adopted by the alien Monarch?

2011-10-09 David Stockhoff replying to larry miller #

Argues that Typhon's remark "I was not born" in The Sword of the Lictor has a simpler explanation than alien origin: he was born through a surrogate mother, as Silk was. Supports this by comparing Macbeth's prophecy "not of woman born" and its fulfilment by a cesarean birth, suggesting Typhon may also have been vat-grown or engineered.
proposes · source-and-allusion · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon was born via surrogate mother like Silk; Typhon was vat-grown or genetically designed

A simpler explanation of Typhon's remark is that he was born as Silk was born, i.e., from a surrogate mother.

Recall Macbeth, where the witches' prophecy "not of woman born" was fulfilled by a cesarean birth. Surely Typhon outdoes Macduff (we are not much impressed by c-sections today) but probably it doesn't need to be by much. Perhaps he was grown in a vat and "decanted" or even genetically "designed," but he'd still need a human body to parasitize.

---
http://www.avast.com

2011-10-09 Larry Miller replying to David Stockhoff #

Proposes that the body Typhon occupies was grown from an embryo or one of Jonas's beans and filled with downloaded consciousness after his original body died. Recalls Typhon's remarks on the longevity of physical bodies versus consciousness and asks what his comment about sending his thoughts out means, citing no passage directly.
proposes · puzzle-solving · theories: Typhon's body grown from embryo or bean

Or maybe that particular body of Typhons was grown from an embryo (Or one of Jonas beans) and then downloaded into it when his original body died? I dont have the book on hand but I also seem to remember him commenting on the longevity of physical bodies in contrast to the longevity of consciousness. Perhaps this wasn't in reference to human bodies but to his original alien form. Any thoughts on his other comment about sending his thoughts out?

Whole message, with quotes · original

died?  I dont have the book on hand but I also seem to remember him
commenting on the longevity of physical bodies in contrast to the
longevity of consciousness.  Perhaps this wasn't in reference to human
bodies but to his original alien form.  Any thoughts on his other
comment about sending his thoughts out?

2011-10-09 Andrew Mason #

Disputes the idea that Typhon is the Neighbor Windcloud, arguing that the main evidence, a shared doubled body part, fails because Typhon's doubled part is artificial. Responds to Larry Miller's question about Borski's theory that Typhon is the Neighbor.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon is the Neighbor Windcloud

I think the main evidence for Typhon as Neighbour was that they both have doubled body parts; but I don't find this plausible, as Typhon's doubled part was artificial.

Whole message, with quotes · original
Larry Miller wrote:

> SO does anyone else give credence to Borskis idea that Typhon is the
> nieghbor Windcloud?

I think the main evidence for Typhon as Neighbour was that they both
have doubled body parts; but I don't find this plausible, as Typhon's
doubled part was artificial.

2011-10-09 Andrew Mason replying to Andrew Mason #

Argues against the idea that Typhon's name marks him as alien, claiming that Commonwealth naming conventions, where saints' names mark people born in the Commonwealth region, point to a human origin. Supports this by citing Ash, Loyal, Baldanders, Mamelta, Roger, and Typhon's daughter Cilinia, whose saint's name is said to imply humanity.
disputes · close-textual-reading · source-and-allusion · theories: Typhon is human, not alien, based on naming

I don't think it does, really. It's not humans, as such, but Commonwealth citizens who have saint's names; Ash, for instance, or Loyal to the Group of Seventeen don't. (Also Baldanders seems to be human in origin but to have adopted a monstrous name when he became a monster.) And this principle is carried on into Long Sun/Short Sun; people born in the Commonwealth region on Urth (Mamelta, Roger) have saints' names, though no one else does. Also, here Typhon's daughter has a saint's name - Cilinia - which seems to imply that she is human.

I don't know if Typhon is an Urthling, or an off-earth human from one of the planets settled during the first empire; but I don't think he's totally alien.

Whole message, with quotes · original
Larry Miller wrote:
>
> On first read I imagined Typhon as human native of Urth. ?But Wolfes
> naming strategum points at Typhon being of alien origin.

I don't think it does, really. It's not humans, as such, but
Commonwealth citizens who have saint's names; Ash, for instance, or
Loyal to the Group of Seventeen don't. (Also Baldanders seems to be
human in origin but to have adopted a monstrous name when he became a
monster.)  And this principle is carried on into Long Sun/Short Sun;
people born in the Commonwealth region on Urth (Mamelta, Roger) have
saints' names, though no one else does. Also, here Typhon's daughter
has a saint's name - Cilinia - which seems to imply that she is human.

I don't know if Typhon is an Urthling, or an off-earth human from one
of the planets settled during the first empire; but I don't think he's
totally alien.

2011-10-10 Jeff Wilson replying to larry miller #

Argues that Typhon, if he was a clone who could move his consciousness at will, would not need a head graft, and proposes that his disclaimer of being born refers to a surgically assembled nature. Supports this by comparing his distant thoughts to speaking machines in the Matchin Tower and Valeria's reception hall, which could serve as telepresences.
proposes · close-textual-reading · theology-and-religion · theories: Typhon's disclaimer of birth refers to surgical assembly; Typhon uses speaking machines as telepresences

If Typhon was a clone to begin with, and could transfer his consciousness at will, why bother with the head graft? Just possess another clone brother.

Consequently, I believe that Typhon's disclaimer of being born must have been originally a reference to his then surgically assembled nature. His thoughts being sent into far places could easily have been him spying through the remaining technological outposts similar to the speaking machines in the Matchin Tower and Valeria's reception hall. He may have had telepresences there to keep his ruling face au courant.

Whole message, with quotes · original

> longevity of consciousness.  Perhaps this wasn't in reference to human
> bodies but to his original alien form.  Any thoughts on his other
> comment about sending his thoughts out?

If Typhon was a clone to begin with, and  could transfer his

machines in the Matchin Tower and Valeria's reception hall. He may have 
had telepresences there to keep his ruling face au courant.

2011-10-10 Larry Miller replying to Jeff Wilson #

Argues that Typhon planned to download his consciousness into another clone body but something prevented it, so the Mandragora seen at the end of the Citadel is likely that discarded clone body. Responds to Jeff Wilson's point that Typhon's far-off thoughts may be spying through remaining technological devices, calling that point interesting.
proposes · puzzle-solving · chronology-and-plot-logic · theories: Mandragora is Typhon's discarded clone body; Typhon planned to download into another clone

I Believe that was Typhons plan to download into another clone body but something happened which prevented this. The Mandragora we see at the end of Citadel is probably this discarded clone body. Your point about Typhon using communication devices is interesting though.

Whole message, with quotes · original
I Believe that was Typhons plan to  download into another clone body
but something happened which prevented this.  The Mandragora we see at
the end of Citadel is probably this discarded clone body.  Your point
about Typhon using communication devices is interesting though.

2011-10-10 David Stockhoff replying to larry miller #

Argues that Typhon could be an alien mind occupying a human body, a possibility its author finds consistent with the Long Sun universe and Wolfe's work generally. Responds to Larry Miller's suggestion that Typhon's bodies were grown from embryos and his mind downloaded, noting that no one had proposed the alien-mind idea or human-like beans before.
proposes · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon is an alien mind in a human body

I'm not sure anyone has suggested before that Typhon is an alien mind in a human body (who for some reason is very attached to his body's face). Nor that the "beans" are human/oid embryos. It seems possible and not inconsistent with the BotNS universe or Wolfe in general.

We do know (or suspect) that the sea-Mothers on Blue and Urth are physical sisters. Moving minds ought to be easier than bodies.

---
http://www.avast.com

2011-10-10 David Stockhoff replying to Jeff Wilson #

Disputes Larry Miller's proposal that Typhon's denial of being born refers to a surgically assembled, cloned nature, arguing that mind-transfer would make the head graft unnecessary. Grounds the objection in the uncertain fatality of mind-scanning in The Fifth Head of Cerberus, Typhon's reluctance to tolerate a second copy of himself, and the fact that Pas was created by scanning.
disputes · chronology-and-plot-logic · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon's disclaimer of being born refers to his surgical assembly; Typhon's head graft is needed despite mind-transfer

Except that this would require "outposts" at the south pole and under the sea.

True that any embryo-implanting technology should encompass cloning, since we know cloning was used to create the uhlans, and therefore the head graft would seem to be rendered unnecessary by mind-transfer technology. However, we don't know for sure whether mind-scanning is fatal (as in TFHoC) or not, and Typhon is not a guy who would want a second Typhon running around even briefly. Doesn't that make it a little less simple? (As opposed to godlike possession powers, which clearly are not called for here---we know Pas was created by scanning.)

I don't think the head graft could possibly be taken as a form of "birth" in any sense. Something more like actual birth is meant.

---
http://www.avast.com

2011-10-10 David Stockhoff replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Typhon was not born in the ordinary way, reading his denial in the Citadel-era dialogue with Severian as a rejection of birth itself rather than of his two-headed state. Builds the case on the text: Typhon treats his body as a steed and Piaton's head as grafted onto him, so only an unusual origin fits the wording. Responds to Jeff Wilson's point about cloning and mind-transfer by working from the quoted dialogue.
disputes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Typhon was not born in the usual manner; Typhon thinks of himself as riding a steed

On rereading, it is somewhat ambiguous---

"No, I was not born as I am, or born at all, as you meantit. Nor was Piaton grafted to me. I was grafted to him. "

---but still leads to only one clean conclusion. Typhon is referring, with "as I am" to his apparent two-headed condition, since Severian plainly asked about it, but "or born at all, as you meantit" must be an aside referring to something Severian did not refer to except with the word "born" itself. Severian has no reason to think Typhon was NOT born, so his assumption is the usual kind of birth (but including c-section as a matter of course, since he is familiar with it). In addition he asks how Typhon got that way.

But Typhon does not think of his current configuration, with his head on a whole body, as "himself" but rather as his riding a "steed." He means:

---I was not born with no body of my own
---Furthermore, I was not born at all, with body or not

The rest is a response to Severian's "was Piaton's head attached to you." Thus, Typhon was not originally born in the usual manner.

Any other interpretation would require that Typhon thinks of himself as two-headed. He doesn't. If he did, he would mean:

---I was not born two-headed
---I was surgically made two-headed

So that's not what he means.

---
http://www.avast.com

2011-10-10 Larry Miller replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Typhon is an alien whose original body was a megatherian, not Piaton, and that Jonas's unfinished beans story in The Piteous Gate hints at cloning from a frozen embryo. Builds on a passage David Stockhoff quoted about Typhon's birth and grafting, citing the links between Scylla and Typhon and Wolfe's naming strategum as support.
proposes · source-and-allusion · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon is an alien; Typhon's original body was a megatherian; Jonas's bean story hints at cloning from a frozen embryo

Yeah the passage is really ambiguous. Ive just been stuck on the idea that Typhon was an alien since rereading BotNS the second time and learning later of Wolfes naming strategum cemented the idea in my mind. All we know for sure is that Piaton is not Typhons original body. The links between Scylla and Typhon suggest that maybe his original form was that of a megatherian. I think Jonas telling the beans story in the same chapter of the Piteous Gate (most likely caused by the undines swimming through the river beyond the wall) is Wolfes sly way of hinting at a connection. Growing from a bean as being a way to explain cloning from a frozen embryo seems to fit with high technology passed off as magic throughout the books. The fact that Wolfe leaves Jonas story unfinished is another big sign that readers are supposed to speculate on these things.

2011-10-10 Jeff Wilson replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Typhon could learn about the south pole and the sea through orbital sensors and machine intelligence rather than physical outposts, using Jonas as an example of a machine that knows about the great beasts. Responds to David Stockhoff's objection that such knowledge would require outposts at the south pole and under the sea, and suggests Jonas was gestated mechanically like Dr Talos.
disputes · close-textual-reading · source-and-allusion · theories: Typhon learns of the south pole via orbital satellite; Jonas is a machine that knows the great beasts; Jonas was mechanically gestated like Dr Talos

I don;t know about require; sensors can have ranges and their data may be used to infer details beyond those. An ancient but still functional satllite in orbit above the south pole and the sea could be accessible to Typhon "Hmm, weather patterns continue to deteriorate... Great Me, what is that THING on the infrared?"

The talking machines could intercept intelligence from the Asciians' transmissions and preserve it for their master's review. Jonas, for example, is a machine that knows about the great beasts without being currently at the south pole or under the sea. He probably lost his antennas for far communication, though.

As to not being born at all, that could mean he was gestated mechanically, perhaps like Dr Talos.

2011-10-10 David Stockhoff replying to larry miller #

Argues that Typhon and Jonas are twins in the sense that both physically ride other bodies, so Typhon could ride bodies like Jonas does with Miles. Grounds this in Jonas riding Miles without visible technology, and in the contrast that Jonas cares little for his body while Typhon cares a lot.
proposes · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon and Jonas are twins who ride bodies; Typhon was an alien in original form

Jonas and Typhon are twins in the sense that they both physically ride other bodies. Jonas does appear to then ride another body (Miles) without any visible technological means of doing so. The door is thus opened for Typhon to do the same. But Jonas clearly cares little for his "body." Typhon definitely cares a lot.

---

>> with "as I am" to his apparent two-headed condition, since Severian
>> plainly asked about it, but "or born at all, as you meantit" must be an
>> aside referring to something Severian did not refer to except with the
>> word "born" itself. Severian has no reason to think Typhon was NOT born,
>> so his assumption is the usual kind of birth (but including c-section as
>> a matter of course, since he is familiar with it). In addition he asks
>> how Typhon got that way.
>>
>> But Typhon does not think of his current configuration, with his head on
>> a whole body, as "himself" but rather as his riding a "steed." He means:
>>
>> ---I was not born with no body of my own
>> ---Furthermore, I was not born at all, with body or not
>>
>> The rest is a response to Severian's "was Piaton's head attached to
>> you." Thus, Typhon was not originally born in the usual manner.
>>
>> Any other interpretation would require that Typhon thinks of himself as
>> two-headed. He doesn't. If he did, he would mean:
>>
>> ---I was not born two-headed
>> ---I was surgically made two-headed
>>
>> So that's not what he means.

2011-10-10 Larry Miller replying to Gerry Quinn #

Proposes that Typhon was originally one of the sea-dwelling Megatherians, with a humanoid bean-grown body he later transferred into, and that his humanoid head was grafted onto Piaton's body. Grounds this on Scylla's mention in Dr. Talos alongside Abaia and Erebus, and on Typhon's hostility toward his waterbound kin, responding to Gerry Quinn's and Larry Miller's arguments.
proposes · puzzle-solving · source-and-allusion · theories: Typhon was originally a Megatherian; Typhon grafted his head onto Piaton's body; Typhon enslaves his waterbound brethren

In dr. Talos play Scylla is mentioned alongside Abaia and Erebus. Knowing that Scylla is part of Typhons family it doesn't seem too farfetched to assume Typhon was originally one of the Megatherians. Maybe his bean grown body was humanoid and he transferred into it from his monster form. Then something prevented him from transferring into the Mandragora in Citadel (more than likely a Proto-Chem) and his only alternative was to graft his humanoid head onto Piatons body. We know that Typhon as Pas did not get on well with Scylla and the others so it makes sense that he would want to enslave them when he regains control of the Commonwealth. Typhon more than likely feels as though he has surpassed his waterbound brethren and does not count himself among them.

Whole message, with quotes · original

control of the Commonwealth.  Typhon more than likely feels as though
he has surpassed his waterbound brethren and does not count himself
among them.

2011-10-10 David Stockhoff replying to Jeff Wilson #

Argues that Typhon should be read as a human-built figure of pure hubris rather than a god or demigod, comparing him to a tower built to reach Heaven. Supports this by pointing to genetics as the explanation for telepathic characters such as Silk, Mucor and Typhon, and says the satellite-surveillance idea in the quoted post is possible but asks too much of the reader.
proposes · theology-and-religion · theories: Typhon is a human-built figure of hubris; Telepathy is a genetic trait explaining Silk, Mucor and Typhon

It's possible. It's just a lot to expect the reader to guess. And Typhon, by his own account, is not one to speak indirectly. For myself, I tend to want to interpret him as neither god nor demigod but as an example of pure hubris. He's like a tower built to reach Heaven. That requires him to have been constructed by humans. Genetics (assuming we allow a gene for telepathy, as we must if we allow telepathy, which we must because Mucor exists) explains Silk, Mucor, and Typhon.

---
http://www.avast.com

2011-10-10 Gerry Quinn replying to larry miller #

Replies to Larry Miller that Typhon's original body may have been a sea creature, not a human, citing the Scylla links and the bean-growing story in The Piteous Gate as a hint at cloning. Responds to Miller's objection by pointing to the beans as embryos and to Wolfe's remark about Damon Knight growing him from a bean.
disputes · source-and-allusion · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon's original form was a megatherian; Bean-growing story hints at Typhon's cloning

From: larry miller

But we know Typhon's head was human or humanoid, and that he lived on land when he ruled Urth. I don't think it's tenable to identify him with a sea monster. Nor was his observation "there are powers in the sea that would rule" followed by "used to be one myself".

In the two later series there is considerable evidence that Typhon was human or at least reasonably humanoid.

The beans certainly are embryos of some kind (or nano-factories or whatever sea monsters develop from). And Wolfe once observed that Damon Knight grew him from a bean. But I don't where you find a connection with Typhon here.

- Gerry Quinn

Whole message, with quotes · original
From: larry miller

> All we know for sure is that Piaton is not Typhons original
> body.  The links between Scylla and Typhon suggest that maybe his
> original form was that of a megatherian.

But we know Typhon's head was human or humanoid, and that he lived on land 
when he ruled Urth.  I don't think it's tenable to identify him with a sea 
monster.  Nor was his observation "there are powers in the sea that would 
rule" followed by "used to be one myself".

In the two later series there is considerable evidence that Typhon was human 
or at least reasonably humanoid.

> I think Jonas telling the
> beans story in the same  chapter of the Piteous Gate (most likely
> caused by the undines swimming through the river beyond the wall)  is
> Wolfes sly way of hinting at a connection.  Growing from a bean as
> being a way to explain cloning from a frozen embryo seems to fit with
> high technology passed off as magic throughout the books.

The beans certainly are embryos of some kind (or nano-factories or whatever 
sea monsters develop from).  And Wolfe once observed that Damon Knight grew 
him from a bean.  But I don't where you find a connection with Typhon here.

- Gerry Quinn

2011-10-10 David Stockhoff replying to António Pedro Marques #

Argues that if Typhon's superpowers are genetic, transferring his mind into another brain would lose them unless the receiving body shares that genetic basis, and suggests this explains his reluctance to lose his face. Responds to Jeff Wilson's claim that duplication involves no transfer of conscience, offering a scanning-and-upload variant instead.
proposes · genre-and-craft · theories: Typhon's superpowers are genetic and non-transferable

Unless there is scanning of the older clone and uploading into the younger.

If Typhon has superpowers that are genetic in origin, he'd lose them if he transferred to another brain, unless that brain also has that genetically-derived infrastructure. Might this explain his reluctance to lose his "face"?

2011-10-10 Gerry Quinn replying to larry miller #

Disputes Larry Miller's idea that Typhon was originally a Megatherian monster, arguing instead that Typhon looked like an ordinary human and needed only a full-body transplant. Responds to Miller's point that Scylla the sea monster and Cilinia, Typhon's daughter, are different entities, and that the beans symbolised sea monster embryos.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon was an ordinary human needing body transplant

From: larry miller

Scylla (the sea monster) and Cilinia (Typhon's daughter whose digitised personality was later named Scylla) are different entities.

The beans were or symbolised the embryos of the sea monsters. Typhon may well have come from off-Urth, but IMO there's nothing to suggest he was visibly distinct from the average human. He got old or ill and needed a full-body transplant, that's all.

2011-10-10 Larry Miller replying to António Pedro Marques #

Argues that Typhon's obsession with his face is about maintaining his rule, with the face serving as a symbol of reign like the Autarch's face on the coin Vodalus gives Severian. Supports this by recalling Typhon's remark that it is the face men obey, and links his hiding in his mountain in The Urth of the New Sun to fear that subjects would overthrow a two-headed alien ruler.
proposes · close-textual-reading · source-and-allusion · theories: Typhon's face is a symbol of his reign

I think Typhons obssession with his face has more to do with maintaining his rule. His face was probably a symbol of his reign in the same way that the Autarchs face adorns the coin given to Severian by Vodalus. I believe he says something along the lines of "Its the face that men obey". This might also explain Typhons hiding out in his mountain in Urth of the New Sun. If the populace were to know they were ruled by a two headed alien they might threaten Typhons monarchy.

Argues that Typhon cannot be one of the Neighbours, since Wolfe has said he did not know about the inhumi, which a figure coming from Blue would likely have known. disputes · author-and-biography · theories: Typhon is not a Neighbour

2011-10-10 Jeff Wilson replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Typhon's mental powers are not limited to his own body, suggesting he could impress his brain's pattern on another mind, as he seems to do when Severian feels gripped during an oath. Grounds this in Typhon's control of Mt Typhon's machines by word and his ability to let Severian see distant parts of Urth, responding to David Stockhoff's point that such a reading asks too much of readers.
proposes · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon impresses his brain pattern on others

But neither is he given to elaborate for the benefit of others.

It's not so much to guess; Mt Typhon itself is such an outpost and Typhon is master of its numerous machines by word. Since he allows Severian to transcend distance and "see" distant parts of the Urth as if present above them, it's not such a stretch to think that he can do that for himself and more besides when he's not busy putting on a show to secure his hold on the unsuspecting Conciliator.

The nature of his mental powers might include the ability to impress his brain's pattern upon another. He does this a bit, when Sev feels his mind gripped by a vise when Typhon tries to make him submit to an oath.

Responds to a quoted passage in which Typhon's nature is treated as god or demigod, pointing to Typhon's mind-reading and the spell he casts on Severian to swear loyalty.
proposes · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon's psionic talents are possible for humans

From: David Stockhoff <[email]>

To: The Urth Mailing List <[email]>

...

No matter how or whether Typhon sent his thoughts to far places, we know he's telepathic.? He makes Severian see through his eyes, and he casts a spell to try to get Severian to swear loyalty to him.? Severian compares this spell to Decuman's, greatly in Typhon's favor.

:-)

With no evidence, I imagine the sea monsters cold-blooded, so they wouldn't show up on the IR.

I'm inclined to agree.

Do we know Mucor's talent is genetic?? Maybe it's the result of an implant or? brain surgery or magic performed on her embryo.? Or are you using "genetic" as a shorthand for some broader idea?

It seems to me Typhon's psionic talents are quite possible for humans in Briah, considering Mucor and Decuman.? We have no idea what enhancement and special training Typhon might have.

Jerry Friedman

2011-10-10 António Pedro Marques replying to Jeff Wilson #

Restates Jeff Wilson's argument that Typhon's clone-copying involves only duplication, not any transfer of consciousness, so the original gains nothing when a copy survives. It replies to the earlier question of why Typhon would need a head graft if he could simply possess a clone brother, quoting Wilson's answer as the basis for the point.
supports · puzzle-solving · theories: Typhon's consciousness is duplicated, not transferred

Jeff Wilson wrote (10-10-2011 08:30):

There is no transferral of conscience. There is just duplication. If you duplicate it and the original dies, the original doesn't get anything from the copy's still being around.

Whole message, with quotes · original
Jeff Wilson wrote (10-10-2011 08:30):
> If Typhon was a clone to begin with, and  could transfer his consciousness
> at will, why bother with the head graft? Just possess another clone brother.

There is no transferral of conscience. There is just duplication. If you 
duplicate it and the original dies, the original doesn't get anything from 
the copy's still being around.

2011-10-10 David Stockhoff replying to António Pedro Marques #

Argues that a copy inherits the original's memories from the point of scanning and so continues as the same entity, citing Home Fires, and that the problem of meeting yourself is solved by treating scanning as death. Responds to António Pedro Marques's remark that the older dies regardless of being copied, and notes that Typhon's loyalty and command, and telepathic presence beyond the face, could explain the face imagery.
disputes · theology-and-religion · theories: Typhon's face conveys presence through telepathy

I don't follow. The younger clone would start off his memories at the point where the older was scanned and thus continue on as the same entity. At least that's how it works in Home Fires. (The problem would be meeting yourself, which would be resolved in a clone situation by assuming scanning = death.)

Yes. Typhon says something about loyalty or command, and that makes sense. But it's not just the face that conveys presence---especially if you are telepathic. So who knows.

2011-10-11 Jeff Wilson replying to Jerry Friedman #

Argues that Typhon's 'seeing through his eyes' in the sled scene is a figure of speech, since the viewing happens through the pupils sculpted into his mountainous likeness rather than telepathy. Responds to Jerry Friedman by citing the mountain's sculpted eyes, the limits of a face on a curved Urth, and a comparison of Typhon to Alexander.
disputes · close-textual-reading · chronology-and-plot-logic · theories: Typhon's eyes are the sculpted pupils of his mountain likeness

Typhon has some mental and some technological effects going in that scene, it's debateable which particular element arises from which origin. However, the seeing through his eyes part is overtly a figure of speech, because they are looking out through the pupils sculpted into Typhon's mountainous likeness. It *might* be a double-meaning, but Typhon's facial eyes can't see over the curvature of the Urth any more than Sev's can.

http://www.flickr.com/photos/yoshihirata/

http://www.sciencedaily.com/releases/2007/10/071015215148.htm

Typhon's got loads of hubris, but he's not all false pride; like Alexander, he did accomplish things no other man approached.

2011-10-11 Tim O'Donnell replying to Andrew Mason #

Proposes that Mucor's genetically arising mental powers, and her birth from an embryo bought by Blood, point to Typhon being a product of genetic breeding or in-vitro rearing rather than ordinary birth. Grounds the idea in Typhon's remark that he was not born as such and in the embryo-breeding evidence around Mucor, while openly calling it conjecture.
proposes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Typhon was raised in vitro; Typhon was a product of genetic breeding; Typhon was once a slave

I think that Mucor is a clue to the nature of Typhon. Mucor has mental powers that arise genetically and she
was born from an embryo purchased by Blood. That embryo must derive from the time of Typhon. We don't know
whether Mucor was created through selective breeding or genetic manipulation or both. I don't think it matters though,
we have evidence that mental powers are associated with embryos bred for the purposes. Is this what Typhon means
when he says he was not born as such? He may have been raised in vitro.

Which only raises more questions about Typhon's origins to me. Was he perhaps a slave himself, the product of a genetic
breeding program? I could go on but I know I am in the realm of sheer conjecture at this point.

Whole message, with quotes · original

breeding program?  I could go on but I know I am in the realm of sheer
conjecture at this point.

2011-10-11 David Stockhoff replying to Jerry Friedman #

Argues that Mucor's psionic talent is fundamentally genetic, not the product of surgery or magic, and that the same holds for Typhon's abilities. Responds to Jerry Friedman by citing the councilors' discussion of Mucor and the missing embryo that was Silk as evidence that the two are much alike.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Mucor's psionic talent is genetic; Typhon's abilities share Mucor's genetic origin

The councilors' discussion of Mucor and the missing embryo that was Silk makes it very clear that they are much the same. Surgery can't explain a "magic" embryo. But the very fact that it's an embryo is a clear signal that the origin of its magic is fundamentally genetic.

> implant or  brain surgery or magic performed on her embryo.  Or are 
> you using "genetic" as a shorthand for some broader idea? It seems to 
> me Typhon's psionic talents are quite possible for humans in Briah, 
> considering Mucor and Decuman.  We have no idea what enhancement and 
> special training Typhon might have. Jerry Friedman

The councilors' discussion of Mucor and the missing embryo that was Silk 
makes it very clear that they are much the same. Surgery can't explain a 
"magic" embryo. But the very fact that it's an embryo is a clear signal 
that the origin of its magic is fundamentally genetic.

2011-10-11 David Stockhoff replying to Jeff Wilson #

Argues that Typhon must be fully human, offering Alexander-style sport and Mule-style mutation as valid models while rejecting Hercules-style semi-divinity and Typhon as an angelic god. Responds to Jeff Wilson's point that Typhon's hubris included real accomplishments, and notes the Tower analogy does not imply failure, since sabotage suggests it would have succeeded.
supports · puzzle-solving · source-and-allusion · theories: Typhon is fully human; Typhon resembles Alexander, not Hercules; Typhon is not an angelic or divine being

Definitely---I don;t mean to imply falseness or failure with the Tower analogy. (I'm sure the Tower would have succeeded if it hadn't been messed with, and in fact the way it was messed with suggests that it would have succeeded, or else why sabotage it?)

Interestingly, Alexander was not constructed---he was clearly a "sport" if not exactly a mutation as the Mule explicitly was. In fact, these are two competing and valid models, but both require that Typhon be human, which was my basic point.

A third model (which I reject) might be Hercules (with whom Alexander was associated by his propagandists, but falsely of course), who was only half-human. His powers were therefore semi-divine, from Up There. The fourth (which I also reject) is that Typhon is some kind of Wolfean angelic being or god---but he's no Hiero. There's no room for more gods in Briah!

---
http://www.avast.com

2011-10-11 David Stockhoff replying to Gerry Quinn #

Argues that Typhon's telepathic and mind-seizing powers in The Book of the New Sun should be read as super-telepathy rather than digital or mind-transfer techno-magic, and that the text needs to explain why it hints at them. Responds to Jerry Friedman's point about Typhon's spell and Severian's comparison to Decuman, rejecting the idea that the powers are gods-based and proposing the monarch-loyalty emblem reading as compatible with them.
disputes · close-textual-reading · genre-and-craft · theories: Typhon's powers are super-telepathy not digital techno-magic; Typhon as emblem of loyalty to the monarch

I'm quite baffled by this. Genetic engineering is techno-magic. Its existence raises questions about cloning and how it works. Mind transfer is techno-magic, whether mechanical or organic. There is no evidence that Typhon had digital-mental techno-magic or that Abaia uses it too.

This is an SF novel. How can it not matter which kind of techno-magic is involved? Why go with the one that has the least evidence for it?

In some sense, yes. We certainly can't disprove that, and the "origin" of telepathy is plainly backgrounded---we don;t need gods or such to explain it. It's just there, and we only need an explanation of how different characters got super-versions of it. Your explanation is the simplest one (see above).
This is certainly a debatable point. But I return to the narrator's purpose: why drop such strong and "classical" hints that Typhon can send his mind forth to commune with aliens under the sea, and that he can seize other's minds, in a universe where
telepathic/projection/possession powers are demonstrated to exist, without accounting for the fact that the alert reader will proceed to interpret this as "super-telepathic powers"? This needs explanation, but there is none.

Possibly Typhon is meant to be an emblem of "loyalty to the monarch"---in other words, a reference to the degrees of loyalty one step below "loyalty to the throne." But this does not contradict Typhon's powers, it just gives deeper significance to his claims that his face is what will get him back his empire.

---
http://www.avast.com

2011-10-11 David Stockhoff replying to Jeff Wilson #

Argues that Typhon's knowledge of powers in the seas comes only from his own thought reaching far places after he awoke, not from any talk with monsters in the abyss. Responds to Jeff Wilson by quoting Typhon's speech and noting that the text gives no evidence for any other source of information.
answers · close-textual-reading

Typhon: "Yes. But I have lived almost a day and have sent my thought into far places. There are powers in the seas now who would rule. They will become our slaves, and the hordes of the north are theirs."

(1) Typhon has learned this information since he awoke (2) by sending his thought into far places. There is no evidence for anything else.

2011-10-11 David Stockhoff replying to Gerry Quinn #

Argues that Typhon's knowledge of distant events in The Book of the New Sun comes from technologically mediated communication rather than innate telepathy, citing his machines and the Mucor danger. Responds to Gerry Quinn's quoted point by Stockhoff, granting that the chapter gives no hard reason to prefer innate telepathy over enhanced telepathy while rejecting the idea that Typhon simply called up a distant server.
disputes · close-textual-reading · genre-and-craft · theories: Typhon's knowledge comes from machines rather than innate telepathy

Impossible! You are well known to be both alert and UNreasonable. ;) See my quote of it in my last post. It's pretty straightforward. I agree that it was indeed technologically mediated but not in the sense you mean.

Yes, it's possible that Typhon mentally called up his old AOL account at the servers on Lune (a "far place") and got a quick update, but there is simply no support for this except that WE have some of this technology and can easily imagine the rest. Its familiarity to us is no argument---it exists but is it Typhon's method? Why should it be?

We know he has machines on the mountain and in Nessus but neither of these is a "far place," let alone more than one far place. We know of no other places where he may have machines; he probably does, but how would these machines know any of the information he states? This is what I mean by "complicated."

I agree that there is no hard reason as far as that chapter to prefer innate/genetic telepathy over (let's call it) electromechanically enhanced telepathy. However, I give you Mucor.
That is an ever-present danger.

2011-10-11 Gerry Quinn replying to Jerry Friedman #

Argues, supporting Jerry Friedman, that Typhon's telepathy matters less than his charisma and leadership, and that telepathic powers may lie in all human brain tissue. Points to Mucor's engineered embryos and brain surgery, which suppressed and restored her powers, and suggests Pas emerges in Silk as he travels back to Silk's island.
supports · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Telepathy inheres in brain tissue of everyone; Mucor's parents met in a petri dish; Typhon's chief powers were charisma and leadership; Pas comes out in Silk as he returns to the island

From: Jerry Friedman

I don?t read too much into this. Typhon is a powerful and charismatic individual; I wouldn?t be surprised if in the _BotNS_ universe such individuals typically have some sort of telepathic power. Like Jeff, I don?t think it?s important whether he uses telepathy or some kind of techno-magic.

Of course Dr. Crane cannot know the details, but I think we should heed his observation that Mucor?s parents (her genetic parents, that is) met in a petri dish. [I imagine the same applies to Silk.] Presumably the embryos were then genetically or otherwise engineered. We know Mucor?s brain developed in an abnormal fashion, and after brain surgery her powers were suppressed for a time but eventually came back. So I imagine the telepathic powers must inhere in brain tissue, and probably exist to some extent in everyone.

I never saw him as primarily a telepath, but indeed perhaps telepathy was a greater part of his powers than I had thought. But I think his chief powers were charisma, leadership, and military strategy. [That?s why I think a lot of Pas is coming out in SilkHorn as he wanders Blue on his way back to his (Silk?s) island.]

- Gerry Quinn

2011-10-11 David Stockhoff replying to James Wynn #

Disputes the idea that Typhon, the dead-but-active figure, has machines that tracked Urth's politics and sent him updates while he was dormant. Argues no character mentions such machines, that subaquatic devices could not gather political information without human input, and that Typhon never refers to any updates.
disputes · close-textual-reading · chronology-and-plot-logic · theories: Typhon received political updates from machines while dead

Several people think this, to my astonishment. But there are complications, which I have pointed out.

Not the least of which being that no character mentions machines capable of political observations and updates, especially subaquatic ones, and even if they existed they would require human input since politics are human systems. You can't do that with satellites. And why not, then, even years-out-of-date interplanetary updates? Typhon mentions none.

2011-10-11 David Stockhoff replying to Jeff Wilson #

Argues that the powers Typhon senses are plainly Erebus and Abaia, agreeing with Larry that Typhon senses them more than he speaks with them. Responds to Jeff Wilson's point that the text never mentions talking to monsters or an abyss, offering a brief rhetorical reply rather than textual citation.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon's powers are Erebus and Abaia

Larry was right to point out that Typhon probably senses the "powers" more than he "speaks" with them. But they are plainly Erebus and Abaia, or I have never read a word by Wolfe and live under the South Pole in a rusty tin can.

2011-10-11 Jerry Friedman replying to António Pedro Marques #

Admits the post is off Wolfe's subject, sharing a link about fish and infrared light and speculating whether a volcanic vent could explain Erebus's light. Then observes that Wolfe's stories seldom ask readers to feel for animals that cannot talk, citing Our Neighbor by David Copperfield as an example.
chats

The fish is illuminated by an infrared source

http://www.plosbiology.org/article/info:doi/10.1371/journal.pbio.0050277

and I'll bet the beautiful creatures on Flickr are too.? In some of the pictures it seems the source?(the sun) is visible.? So maybe if Erebus swam or oozed over a volcanic vent...

Of course this has nothing to do with Wolfe,?and I don't mind if it turns out I'm wrong.

By the way,?many thanks to Yokogawa et al. for publishing in an open-access journal!

Indeed.? Maybe I can observe that?in Wolfe stories, we're seldom invited to care?about animals that can't talk.? For instance, what would "Our Neighbor, by David Copperfield" be like if anyone suggested we feel sorry for the birds?? Say if a starling said, "I can't get out"?

No doubt there are lots of exceptions, though.

Jerry Friedman

2011-10-11 Gerry Quinn replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Typhon's awareness of undersea creatures may be technologically mediated, such as an archive of news reports, rather than telepathic, and that the narrator's account allows other readings. Responds to Gerry Quinn's challenge about Typhon's apparent mind-projection powers by citing Severian's remark that the loyalty-inducing power must lie in Vodalus's coin, and comparing Typhon with Decuman.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon's undersea awareness is technologically mediated; Typhon's powers can be read non-magically; Narrator's interpretation of events is not the only reading

From: David Stockhoff

I consider myself a reasonably alert reader, and I drew no such conclusion. Indeed, I assumed that Typhon?s awareness of the undersea monsters (did he say that he actually communicated with them, or did he just describe them, in which case he might just have accessed an archive of old news reports?) was technologically mediated.

As for magic and the comparison between Typhon and Decuman, I had meant to mention the coin given to Severian by Vodalus at the start of the story. Recall Severian?s observation that the power to instill loyalty must be present in the coin, because ? as he points out very scientifically ? things act of themselves or not at all.

My conclusion is that there is room for interpreting these events in ways other than Severian would interpret them...

- Gerry Quinn

From: David Stockhoff

> seize other's minds, in a universe where 
> telepathic/projection/possession powers are demonstrated to exist, 
> without accounting for the fact that the alert reader will proceed to 
> interpret this as "super-telepathic powers"? This needs explanation, but 
> there is none.

2011-10-11 Jeff Wilson replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Typhon's ship commanders slipping away unnoticed tells against his having long-range ESP, and that he instead gathers Ascian signal intelligence from automated listening posts. Grounds this in the commanders' escape, the Ascian and beast references, and Vodalus's confirmation of long-distance communication, while answering David Stockhoff's claim that Typhon speaks with Erebus and Abaia.
disputes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Typhon gathers Ascian signal intelligence from listening posts; Typhon's powers are not literal ESP

Typhon's ship commanders slipping away before he knows it argues against his long-range ESP. He mentions the Asciians in the same breath as the beasts and knows they are beholden to them as the old autarch and Vodalus later confirm. This is entirely consistent with him retrieving Asciian signal intelligence from automated listening posts; Vodalus confirms they use long-distance comms.

Typhon's sending of his thoughts into distant places remains a somewhat curious idiom if it is not literal, but it is not entirely without precedent in literature and bombast and other figurative expression.

Whole message, with quotes · original

> or I have never read a word by Wolfe and live under the South Pole in a
> rusty tin can.

later confirm. This is entirely consistent with him retrieving Asciian

use long-distance comms.

Typhon's sending of his thoughts into distant places remains a somewhat

precedent in literature and bombast and other figurative expression.

2011-10-11 Jeff Wilson replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that the Ascians' communications in the Urth books would be ordinary radio, given their use of robots and electric lights, and that Wolfe's characters are often skilled at subterfuge against men and machines. Responds to David Stockhoff's point about signal intelligence by noting there is no hint of a cloak against mind powers, and cites the Athenians line as a clue to Ascian methods.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Ascians use ordinary radio

Skill at subterfuge and confounding men and machines is fairly common in the books but there's no hint of a cloak against mind powers.

I don't know how ordinary it might be, but yes, it assumes that the Ascians use radio along with robots and electric lights.

"I've sent my Athenians into distant places."

2011-10-11 David Stockhoff replying to Jeff Wilson #

Argues that Typhon's knowledge of distant events need not come from telepathy, since it could come from signal intelligence, and questions whether the argument assumes Erebus and Abaia use ordinary radio. Responds to Jeff Wilson's quoted claim that Typhon's commanders slipping away argues against his long-range ESP, pointing out that this objection also undercuts signal intelligence.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon gets distant news through Asciian signal intelligence

Of course, it argues against good signal intelligence as well. This seems to assume Erebus and Abaia use ordinary radio. Right? Its ambiguity-while-seeming-perfectly-clear would not be without precedent in Wolfe either.

2011-10-11 Marc Aramini replying to Tim O'Donnell #

Proposes that Mucor's inherited mental powers, from an embryo bought by Blood, hint at Typhon's nature, suggesting Typhon was raised in vitro or engineered like the exultants. Grounds this in Mucor's origin and Typhon's remark that he was not born as such, and floats the idea that his frozen embryos could be a family for recolonization.
proposes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon was raised in vitro; Typhon was an engineered ubermensch like the exultants; Typhon's frozen embryos are his family

From: Tim O'Donnell <[email]>
Subject: (urth) Typhon's Nature
To: [email]
Date: Tuesday, October 11, 2011, 1:30 AM

I think that Mucor is a clue to the nature of Typhon.? Mucor has mental powers that arise genetically and she was born from an embryo purchased by Blood.? That embryo must derive from the time of Typhon.??We don't know whether Mucor was created through selective breeding or genetic manipulation or both.? I don't think it matters though, we have evidence that mental powers are associated with embryos bred for the purposes.? Is this what Typhon means when he says he was not born as such?? He may have been raised in vitro. ?
Which only raises more questions about Typhon's origins to me.? Was he perhaps a slave himself, the product of a genetic breeding program?? I could go on but I know I am in the realm of sheer conjecture at this point.

?
I think it more likely Typhon was more like an engineered ubermensch like the exultants, but even more special.? And that his little frozen embryos?might be?his family rebooted for a recolonization of the future.?son silk,?Mucor as Scylla?? Even Echidna?? Very hard to say.

2011-10-11 António Pedro Marques replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues against David Stockhoff's view that Typhon would not duplicate his conscience through cloning, saying a younger clone starting from the scan point would continue as the same entity. Responds to Stockhoff's claim that Typhon created Pas as a lieutenant rather than a replacement and his unease about a hidden meaning in the books.
disputes · puzzle-solving · theories: Typhon's clones continue as the same entity

David Stockhoff wrote (11-10-2011 03:47):

The younger clone would be the same entity for everyone, including itself, except for the older clone.

Which I don't think is an option for Typhon. He doesn't get special kicks out of having his conscience duplicated if the original doesn't carry on. Afaict he created Pas as a lieutenant, not as a replacement.

What bothers me is that I think there is something hidden there but I haven't got a clue what, so I'm left with the sensation that maybe there is nothing - and in that case my impression that there is becomes an uncomfortable reminder that I have no clue about the whole books.

2011-10-11 David Stockhoff replying to António Pedro Marques #

Questions whether Severian's report that Typhon grips his mind is invalid, proposing instead that Typhon's machines merely monitored Ascian radio traffic and analyzed it for him. Recalls rereading the 'sent my thought' line and Mucor's existence as apparent evidence of mental powers, arguing that only Briah contains such evidence while the rest may be misleading.
proposes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Typhon's machines monitored Ascian radio traffic; Typhon only convinces people he has powers

Is it possible that Sev's observation that Typhon grips his mind like a vise is simply invalid? And Jeff is right, and Typhon's machines simply monitored Ascian radio traffic for millennia, and they analyzed the data for him?

30 years ago I read TBotNS and read the line "sent my thought" literally. I was suspicious, naturally, since I knew Wolfe, and suspended judgment until I read the above about Typhon gripping Sev's mind. To me, this confirmed that Typhon had limited but super mental powers. Mucor's existence confirmed it further. Yet there was always a nagging thought that something was missing. There seemed no point to Typhon's possessing these abilities. But beyond these two potentially misleading statements, there is no evidence for them---only evidence that they exist in Briah. Is Typhon's power merely that he can convince people he has powers?

Beyond that, the face stuff points to Silk. But as James points out, it's maddening!

---

Argues that Typhon would have set up infrared monitoring because the cooling sun will shift its peak output into infrared wavelengths. Responds to Jerry Friedman's idea of an infrared-lit creature such as Erebus near a volcanic vent, offering satellite infrared images of an estuary as an analogy for seeing a moving shape at that scale.
proposes · theories: Typhon monitors infrared because the sun is cooling

It's still relevant in that the cooling sun will increasingly shift its peak output into the infrared frequencies, so Typhon would have reason to have set up IR monitoring.

2011-10-11 Jeff Wilson replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Abaia and Erebus rule through fear and intimidation of the masses, backed by threats of flooding, blocked harbors and pestilence, and that the beasts learned to communicate with their Urthly minions. Responds to David Stockhoff's question about whether the rulers need ESP powers, suggesting that the undines were bred and modified to communicate with them.
answers · close-textual-reading · theories: Abaia and Erebus rule by fear rather than ESP; Undines were bred to communicate with the beasts

Their minions are the kind of "people" who seek to do their bidding, and they handle ruling over the masses by fear. "Yield, lest we flood your lands/barricade the harbor/blot the sun with smoke and ash/send pestilences, etc." The beasts themselves are smart enough to learn how to communicate with their Urthly minions by voice-like noises, writing, pictures, etc. if they didn't already know it when they got here.

For extra horror, Urthlings like the undines are bred and modified in order to better communicate with their uncanny and cyclopean soverigns.

Whole message, with quotes · original

2011-10-11 Lee Berman replying to Andrew Mason #

Argues that Typhon, Abaia and Erebus really possess mental powers rather than merely persuading people, citing the Mandragora connection and the way these powers are shown operating through agents. Responds to David Stockhoff's suggestion that Severian's sense of Typhon's mind-gripping might be invalid, and notes the text's agents such as Juturna, Idas and Tinkerbell.
disputes · allegory-and-typology · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon's mental powers are real, not mere persuasion

I don't think so. Again, I will invoke Typhon's namesake, the Mandragora. For me

the connection between these characters is nearly undeniable and I find the

prospect of Severian being delusional about both their mental powers to be a very

bleak one in regard to the enjoyment of the story.

Moreover I get the sense that Wolfe's message in this and other works is more that

there is a real component to that we consider magic or mystical or spiritual

rather than the opposite, that being that all mystical and spiritual items are merely

tricks to fool the weak-minded.

I have a nagging feeling the text does make some mention of Abaia controlling humanity

purely through the power of his thought, but I can't pin the passage down.

Anyway, we are explicitly shown another way these guys (and their angelic analog,

Tzadkiel) control and influence humanity, which is through the use of agents. Some

are human, like Ascians, Purn and Severian. Some are more than human, though less than

the great powers- beings like Barbatus and Famulimus and Apheta and (I think) Inire and

the Cumaean. Some agents are human sized and shaped but are produced by being

pinched off from the gigantic bodies of the greater being- characters like Juturna and

Idas, Tinkerbell and (I think) Seawrack.

I don't think we are meant to know the origin of Typhon but his name and Severian's

description of his powers have always suggested to me that he is more than just a

regular human who is simply a good talker.

Whole message, with quotes · original
>David Stockhoff: Is it possible that Sev's observation that Typhon grips his mind 

>like a vise is simply invalid?...Is Typhon's power merely that he can convince 
>people he has powers?

 

I don't think so. Again, I will invoke Typhon's namesake, the Mandragora. For me

2011-10-11 Jeff Wilson replying to David Stockhoff #

Questions whether the beast-gods like Typhon and Abaia really have the mental power to enslave half the planet, since they rely on minions. Responds to David Stockhoff's claim by suggesting enslavement could work through subordinate minions, comparing it to Mao's rule without mind powers.
questions · myth-and-structure

We know they have minions, yes? Abaia has undines, Erebus has perischii. If they had sufficient mental wattage to enslave half the planet, why do they bother with minions? Or is the mind power what they use to control the minions who then enslave half the planet?

Chairman Mao did it without any mind powers at all using 100% pure minion power.

2011-10-11 David Stockhoff replying to Jeff Wilson #

Disputes Jeff Wilson's claim that Typhon's mind-reading and Ascian communication are settled, arguing Typhon is not a pulp mind reader and questioning what 'sending thought' means. Presses the point with questions about Typhon's arrogance toward sea powers, whether his cataphracts can fight underwater, and why he would not sense the sea minds himself.
disputes · close-textual-reading

This is hardly quibbling. Typhon is explicitly not a mind reader in the classic pulp sense. So those comments don't argue much.
No, please just explain your remark. If you've sent your Athenians, presumably they went. What has this to do with "sending thought"? It is indeed a "somewhat curious idiom if it is not literal, but it is not entirely without precedent," as I agreed above.

I pointed out the use of the singular because to some, "thought" might imply more of a focused and intimate connection, while "thoughts" might be interpreted more easily as thought commands to mindreading machines. It may or may not bear directly on the discussion. See below. Although difficult to imagine, especially given what we know of Ascian. I mean, "Abaia says this" and "Erebus directs that" don't reveal much.

Why is Typhon not surprised that there are powers in the sea, of all places? If he is purely an Alexander-style military conqueror, should he not consider fighting and defeating seamonsters a tactical and strategic challenge? Yet his arrogance dismisses them. Do his cataphracts function under water?

If Typhon can send his thought into the sea and sense other great minds there, he would confirm their presence and objectives and weaknesses himself.

2011-10-11 Larry Miller replying to Lee Berman #

Argues that the Mandragora was a proto-chem that Typhon built to download his mind into, citing Silk's discovery in The Book of the Long Sun that gods can possess chems. Supports the idea with Echidna's mention of a Prime Calcula in the Citadel and the Latin meaning of calculus as stone, as a naming pattern in Wolfe's work.
proposes · puzzle-solving · source-and-allusion · theories: The Mandragora was a proto-chem built by Typhon; Typhon downloaded into the Mandragora; Prime Calcula refers to the Mandragora

As I posted before the Mandragora was likely a proto chem Typhon built to download into. Theres a point in Long Sun where Silk having discovered that the Gods can possess chems and I remember Echidna mentioning a Prime Calcula in the Citadel. Im sure this was a reference to the mandragora. This would also keep with Wolfes naming conventions as Calculas is I believe the latin word for stone.

2011-10-12 António Pedro Marques replying to Jerry Friedman #

Replies to Jerry Friedman's point on cold-blooded animals, noting that the body temperature of ectotherms differs from the surroundings only by a little, and it is largely a side discussion of biology rather than Wolfe. Admits the poster may be wrong and says the exchange has nothing to do with Wolfe's books, so it is an off-topic aside on animal physiology.
chats

Jerry Friedman wrote (11-10-2011 23:45):

'Cold-blooded' does not mean the creature is at the same temperature as its environment. It means it has no organic* way to regulate the temperature. Of course, that means it can't stray very far from the environment's temperature, but a little bit is doable.

(*) 'Fever' in mammals means the animal's body produces large amounts of heat. In lizards it means the animal moves into the sun so as to increase the temperature. Imagine that, behavioural fever.

Whole message, with quotes · original
Jerry Friedman wrote (11-10-2011 23:45):
> Of course this has nothing to do with Wolfe, and I don't mind if it turns
> out I'm wrong.

'Cold-blooded' does not mean the creature is at the same temperature as its
environment. It means it has no organic* way to regulate the temperature. Of
course, that means it can't stray very far from the environment's
temperature, but a little bit is doable.

(*) 'Fever' in mammals means the animal's body produces large amounts of 
heat. In lizards it means the animal moves into the sun so as to increase 
the temperature. Imagine that, behavioural fever.

2011-10-12 Larry Miller replying to Marc Aramini #

Argues that the Prime Calcula Echidna mentions to Silk, which the poster connects to the Citadel and Urth, is more likely the mandragora than the object Severian wakes beside in the man-ape mine. Reasons from the context of Echidna's speech after she possesses Maytera Marble and kills Blood, and from Wolfe's habit of capitalizing Citadel.
proposes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Prime Calcula is the mandragora on Urth

Dug out my long sun books last night to reread and found the quote. Echidna has just possessed Maytera Marble and killed Blood. She says in response to Silks question "The Prime Calcula........His Citadel.........". Wolfe always capitalizes the citadel to seperate it from other strongholds so this Prime Calcula must be on Urth. At first I thought this refered to the thing Severian wakes in the mine of the man apes. But the mandragora seemjs to make more sense in the context of Silk and Echidnas conversation.

2011-10-12 David Stockhoff replying to Jeff Wilson #

Argues that telepathic beings such as Typhon would still use minions and ordinary tools to rule, and that fear alone could not subjugate inland peoples. Responds to Jeff Wilson's suggestion that Mao ruled through minion power by pointing out that Mao had cadres and that coastal tribes never invaded inland.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon would not abandon radio and electromechanical technology; Fear of the sea cannot explain inland subjugation

Yeah, but Mao didn't live under the ocean and across the planet! Even he had cadres of minions. Just as a telepathic Typhon would not abandon radio and other electromechanical technology (see the Mule), the Beasts would not abandon basic tools of manipulation.

It seems to me your scheme of frightening humans into submission would only work for coastal peoples. There seems to be no pattern of coastal tribes invading inland, but rather of periscii and ascii, as you say. There must be something more, in addition to ideology and self-interest. Cthulhu only ever used dreams, but then he was not awake.

2011-10-12 David Stockhoff replying to Gerry Quinn #

Argues that Typhon's telepathy is significant not as a pulp trope but because it ties into cloning and Silk, and that Typhon serves as a hyper-anti-Autarch, a Satan to Severian's Christ figure. Grounds this in the blurring of machine and human across Briah, Typhon's first act of feeding Severian and demanding an oath, and the argument that the hints about his powers should inform rather than confuse.
disputes · allegory-and-typology · genre-and-craft · theories: Typhon as hyper-anti-Autarch and Satan to Severian's Christ figure; Mucor's telepathy results from genetic modification

Downplaying mental magic seems pointless if such magic serves the narrative, which it obviously does. There's no question that telepathy is a classic pulp trope that simply must appear in TBotNS, and therefore it appears. Typhon's powers, great or little, came up in a discussion of cloning and Silk---THAT is their critical significance. (Note: Mucor?s telepathic abilities are NOT the result of brain modification but of genetic modification, since they are present in embryo and such embryos are very special.) But there is more.

I agree that various technologies are deliberately blurred, as is (famously) the line between technology and magic. This seems to be both a stylistic approach and a philosophy. For example, in Wolfe, there is little distinction between human and machine. Machines can read minds and project eidolons. Machines can do anything humans can do and vice versa, because both are material and explicitly non-divine. Both can manipulate time and space, because both are material and not divine. This "law" underlies all of Briah. Blurring and ambiguity can hardly be said to weaken the significance of the elements that are blurred---only the significance of the original distinctions.

I see Typhon's role in TBotNS as a sort of hyper-anti-Autarch, a Satan to Severian's parallel/opposite Christ figure, who demands that Severian kneel and accept him as his lord. Sev is practically just out of the womb of the Tower and has left the guild for good when he meets Typhon and is at a very low point. So what's the first thing Typhon does? He feeds Severian and demands an oath of loyalty.

What is wrong with this oath? The more unique Typhon is, the more that oath would be to his person and less to his throne. Typhon is all arrogance and denial of love and death. His military prowess is implied at best, but hints are dropped, in the context of the very SF tradition you call "not uncommon," as to his unique mental powers. What good does it do to drop fake hints? They can mislead or provoke thought or lead to other points, but they should not confuse.

---
http://www.avast.com

2011-10-12 Lee Berman replying to Andrew Mason #

Argues that the Mandragora in The Book of the New Sun is too biological to be the Citadel's prime calcula, and instead proposes that Ossipago might be Father Inire in disguise, linking Inire to Pan and Dionysus. Responds to Larry Miller's claim that the Mandragora is a proto-chem built by Typhon, noting the Latin root of calcula as pebble and the lack of mechanical parts in the fetus.
disputes · source-and-allusion · puzzle-solving · theories: Ossipago is Father Inire in disguise; Inire is the prime calcula of the Citadel; Inire's name connects to Pan and Dionysus

Sorry I missed your post Larry. Some interesting ideas! There are a few Latin words for stones and rocks. "Calculus" is most specifically for "pebble" which became associated with math via the use of pebbles in a Roman abacus-type calculator. Anyway, with both the stone and computing aspects of the word, it becomes a fantastic option as a name for a Chem. Not sure why Wolfe wanted to feminize it by spelling it "calcula".

Pursuing that thought, I can't agree that the Mandragora is a good candidate for the prime calcula of the Citadel. It seems to be a purely biological being; a fetus, immersed in preserving fluid. Severian gives us no hints of any mechanical parts. Rather than being a "prime calcula" it is so ineffectual it just wants to die.

Moreover, I think all of BotNS is contains a subtext of more biological SF, like cloning and chemical memory. It was written in a time before electronic downloads, when computer memory was encoded on magnetic tape and paper punch cards. 5HoC does have Mr. Million but his mental upload was a fatal process and apparently not subject to further up/downloads.

Anyway, if Wolfe meant the "prime calcula" to refer to a chem being in BotNS I think we only have two or three characters who qualify as mechanical beings: Jonas, Sidero and Ossipago. None would seem to have any association with the Citadel, unless.....

Perhaps we could extend some latitude to Borski's idea that Ossipago is Father Inire in disguise. The identification is based largely on the idea of obscured eyes (eyes being the window to the soul, which chems don't have?). The role of "bone grower" and "father" are not so far apart.

If we can accept this idea, we now have a "prime calcula" who has been the power behind the throne of the Commonwealth for a millenium, is the architect of the House Absolute and likely the Citadel (given the mystic pathways between them), and is the boss of Barbatus and Famulimus, directing their "hammer" and "tongs" actions which forge humanity.

Moreover, Inire's name can be connected to the god Pan (from whom Pas seems to have derived his name) and thus Dionysus, who is associated with The Outsider in RTTW. I find some substance to these connections and find them helpful to my own understanding of the text. Thanks for the inspiration, Larry! (even if not shared, heh)

2011-10-12 Lee Berman replying to Andrew Mason #

Argues that readers divide between those who see Wolfe's work as simply entertaining yarns and those who find an overarching philosophy beneath it, which makes discussion difficult. Responds to Andrew Mason's quoted remark that Wolfe deliberately blurs technology and magic, agreeing that a story with spaceships and time travel naturally includes telepathy, and jokes about it.
supports

Yes. I think this cuts to the crux of the debate. For some readers, Wolfe's work seems to be a collection of rippingly good yarns. Any perception of an overarching philosophy and use of sub-text to convey it is seen as a distraction and diminishing of the story. These different approaches create a difficult platform for discussion between parties.

I tend to agree with your assertion that a story which contains spaceships, ray guns, robots, aliens, anti-gravity, cloning and time travel simply has to have telepathy. How could it be left out? ;- )

Whole message, with quotes · original
>David Stockhoff: I agree that various technologies are deliberately blurred, as is 
>(famously) the line between technology and magic. This seems to be both 
>a stylistic approach and a philosophy.
 
Yes. I think this cuts to the crux of the debate. For some readers, Wolfe's work seems
to be a collection of rippingly good yarns. Any perception of an overarching philosophy 
and use of sub-text to convey it is seen as a distraction and diminishing of the story.
These different approaches create a difficult platform for discussion between parties.
 
I tend to agree with your assertion that a story which contains spaceships, ray guns, robots, 
aliens, anti-gravity, cloning and time travel simply has to have telepathy. How could 
it be left out? ;- )

2011-10-12 David Stockhoff replying to Gerry Quinn #

Argues that Typhon's power in the Ascian material comes from his own force of personality rather than from technology, making him an anti-Autarch who governs by unleashing himself. Responds to Gerry Quinn's point about blurred boundaries between charisma and telepathy by contrasting an emperor who inherits wealth with one who asserts self, and by drawing a political analogy to modern presidents.
disputes · theology-and-religion · theories: Typhon is an anti-Autarch whose power comes from himself

Exactly. I just wanted to be clear there, since the brain develops AFTER the genes are set.
Quite possible! Pact with the devil and all that. Manfred, perhaps. See below.
Astounding, or else pedestrian. We can't really tell, can we? But I agree that mechanisms are not strictly critical outside of hard SF, which this ain't. But what about my point above? An interstellar emperor who basically uses Daddy's wealth to create his empire is quite different from one who asserts the power of his self, is it not? (A Trump-style president who uses self-promotion to hide his incompetence vs an Eisenhower?) Force of personality, force of mind, same thing. Fancy electronics---not so much. BIG difference there, and that's a bigger point than ESP, aside from the cloning debate.

Typhon wants to govern the universe by unleashing (ungoverning) himself. That's why I call him anti-Autarch. If his powers come from himself, the point is more strongly made.
