2011-10-20 David Stockhoff replying to Gerry Quinn #

Proposes that the phrase about Nessus being newly built may mean newly rebuilt, and that Typhon gathered books to Nessus to preserve them, not burn them. Argues by comparing Nessus's history to the Library of Alexandria and a centralized library's vulnerability, and asks Andrew whether there is evidence for the monarch's repentance.
proposes · source-and-allusion · close-textual-reading · theories: Typhon gathered books to save them; Nessus was newly rebuilt, not built

I suppose one could interpret that line as "newly REbuilt" ..... there could be some conflation of names and dates in your average Urthian's sense of history. The origin of Nessus itself is so confused it can't be used for reliable dating. But Nessus should surely precede Typhon by a chiliad, except for the Citadel, which I'd connect with the useless stump of Typhon's lost fleet as well as the library.

But recall that Alexander, a precursor to Typhon, built the Library of Alexandria, which later burned. Ironically, if it had not been built it would not have burned. Point being, a centralized library system is just as vulnerable and more subject to restriction than a dispersed or nonsystematic one. The whole story of the collection of books to burn them suggests this. Typhon need not embrace universal knowledge to collect all books and bury them below the ground where only he can access them.

Andrew, do you have evidence for the monarch's repentance? Is it possible that Typhon brought all remaining books to Nessus to save them, where a predecessor burned them?

2011-10-20 Jeff Wilson replying to Gerry Quinn #

Argues that the Nessus passage may be a misattributed or misdated detail, since later rulers often gathered and claimed works of their predecessors, so an apparent chronology problem need not disprove Andrew's theory. Responds to Gerry Quinn's objection that Nessus predates Typhon's reign by citing ancient history and the Cleopatra's Needles as parallels.
disputes · source-and-allusion · chronology-and-plot-logic · theories: Nessus-attribution error explained by later rulers' claims

On the other hand, Wolfe is a keen consumer of ancient histories where it is very commonplace for later rulers to accumulate misattributed works of their predecessors. The same is true of more contemporary history, as witness the various Cleopatra's Needles.

2011-10-20 David Stockhoff replying to Jeff Wilson #

Supports Gerry Quinn's point that later rulers accumulate misattributed works, adding jokes about Napoleon's supposed inventions and how stories shift over centuries. Cites Tolkien's verse version of the Norse sagas on Attila's defeat of the Burgundians, and suggests that lost or telepathically stored records would make the telescoping of history in the thread's question worse.
supports · theories: Lost records would make history telescope further

Exactly. And the more glorious the better---Napoleon, for example, famously invented not only canned food but also Egyptology, the ambulance, the submarine, and so on. (Googling the topic, I find he also invented the computer and proved Napoleon's theorem, whatever that is. Might even be true!)

Having just read Tolkien's verse version of the Norse sagas based on Attila's defeat of the Burgundians, I now have a sharper appreciation of how stories move around and adapt over centuries. If the records were themselves lost---or they were all kept in the telepathic cloud---the telescoping effect could only increase.

2011-10-20 Jeff Wilson replying to Andrew Mason #

Argues that the Citadel's old port could date back many ages, since relativistic rockets landing there would bring late arrivals in later eras, and that the great vault may refer to the subterranean complex extending into the city and to the House Absolute. Responds to Andrew Mason's quoted point about Cyriaca's story and Typhon's actions, offering a reading of the port and vault in the Citadel passages.
proposes · close-textual-reading · chronology-and-plot-logic · theories: the old port dates from many ages

The "old port" could date back many ages, once you establish it as a place for relativistic rockets to land you have to expect late arrivals in later ages, and as kind of useful, low-maintenance wasteland.

The "great vault" can also refer to the subterranean complex that extends into the city and to the House Absolute.

2011-10-20 Andrew Mason replying to Andrew Mason #

Quotes the passage in The Sword of the Lictor where the autarch, after dreaming, orders the book-burning halted and a vault built to house the gathered volumes and scrolls. Offers this text as the answer to Andrew Mason's question about whether the monarch repented and whether Typhon saved the remaining books from a predecessor's burning.
answers · close-textual-reading · theories: The autarch's dream made him spare the books

'But on the night before the burning was to begin, the autarch of the time, who had never dreamed before the wild dreams of sleep but only waking dreams of dominion, dreamed at last.... When morning came, he ordered that the torches not be kindled, but that there should be a great vault built to house all the volumes and scrolls the white-robed men had gathered.' (SOTL 6.)

Argues that Cyriaca's account of Typhon's city cannot be fully accurate, since Typhon's own actions seem to be the source of the story, and notes Jonas places the Citadel's foundation too early. Responds to Gerry Quinn's post, building on his point about the Nessus passage and the dating of the city's founding.
